Classification of Atmospheric Traveling Waves at Cloud Level
Authors: Adrian Constantin, Zhiwu Lin, Hao Zhu
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We classify within the quasi-geostrophic framework all types of traveling waves in zonal bands of the planetary atmosphere at cloud level according to their wave speeds. This classification pertains to waves of all amplitudes, going beyond the small-amplitude perturbative regime; it provides a structurally robust criterion for determining which traveling-wave profiles are dynamically possible. We also show that each wave classification type was observed on Jupiter or Saturn. Building on this classification, we investigate the related rigidity issue for large-amplitude traveling waves and waves propagating near shear flows. Our study offers a unified quantitative characterization of the intrinsic constraints for traveling waves in the quasi-geostrophic regime of planetary atmospheric flow.

Introduction

The two-dimensional incompressible β β-plane equation is widely used to study the dynamics of quasi-geostrophic flows that are confined to zonal bands and for which the vertical motion is negligible. Such flows are predominant in the dynamics of the atmosphere of the gas giants (Jupiter and Saturn) and of the ice giants (Neptune and Uranus) at cloud level. By approximating the Coriolis parameter as varying linearly with latitude, having the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_0+\beta y$$\end{document}f0+βy with1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f_0=\frac{2\varOmega 'R'}{U'}\,\sin \theta _0\quad \text {and}\quad \beta =\frac{2\varOmega 'R'}{U'}\,\cos \theta _0\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f0=2Ω′R′U′sinθ0andβ=2Ω′R′U′cosθ0,the β β-plane equation captures the essential behavior induced by sphericity combined with rotation; here \varOmega 'Ω′ is the constant rate of rotation of the spherical planet of radius R'R′ about its polar axis, U'U′ is the typical magnitude of the horizontal velocity and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _0 \in \big ( -\frac{\pi }{2},\,\frac{\pi }{2}\big )$$\end{document}θ0∈(-π2,π2) is the reference latitude of the flow region that does not comprise the poles (see the discussion in [10, 30] and Table 1). Rather than the more intricate Euler equation in rotating spherical coordinates, the β β-plane equation is formulated in a Cartesian coordinate system, which simplifies the analysis but retains the effects of sphericity at leading order for the bands of moderate meridional width typical for Jupiter and Saturn [48]. The atmospheric flow at cloud level on Neptune and Uranus has also a zonal structure but the bands are much wider than those on Jupiter and Saturn: both ice giants have only two major prograde jets, one per hemisphere, with retrograde equatorial jets [45]. Moreover, the latitudinally averaged zonal flow is stable on Neptune and Uranus (see [6]), a feature which impedes the formation of long-lasting wave perturbations, in marked contrast to the gas giants.Table 1Values of the relevant parameters for Jupiter and SaturnPlanetR'R′Ω 'Ω′ U'U′Jupiter69911 km\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1.76\times 10^{-4}$$\end{document}1.76×10-4 rad/s150 m/sSaturn58232 km\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1.62\times 10^{-4}$$\end{document}1.62×10-4 rad/s150 m/s

Values of the relevant parameters for Jupiter and Saturn

The banded appearance of Jupiter and Saturn is primarily caused by their rapid rotation. Both giant planets exhibit east–west jet streams along the boundaries of these zonal bands, with speeds ranging up to 150 m s\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{-1}$$\end{document}-1 on Jupiter and up to 400 m s\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{-1}$$\end{document}-1 on Saturn. These jets dominate the dynamics, containing most of the kinetic energy at the cloud tops on Saturn and about 90% on Jupiter [41], and lead to the formation of zonally propagating long-lived coherent shear flows. A plethora of short-lived cloud structures arise as perturbations of these shear flows but some persistent dynamical features can also be observed. Typically these are traveling waves propagating zonally but occasionally vortices, such as Jupiter’s Great Red Spot (GRS) and Saturn’s polar vortices, also occur. While vortices that persist on large time scales require special circumstances (for example, the GRS has a quite peculiar dynamics unlike that of terrestrial hurricanes [8] and Saturn’s polar vortices are hot spots [42]), a wide range of traveling waves have been identified by telescope and spacecraft (see [39] for the identification of a variety of waves detected on Jupiter by the Juno spacecraft). Such waves are unstable perturbations of an underlying shear flow that grow and acquire specific dynamic characteristics which set them apart from the original state of the flow (e.g. the circumpolar waves that form Saturn’s hexagon – see [9]).

Coherent waves can only propagate azimuthally since the strong jets ensure that waves propagating in a direction tilted to the East–West direction are short-lived. The velocity field of a traveling wave propagating zonally in a band \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_- \le y \le d_+$$\end{document}d-≤y≤d+ at the speed c has the form (u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y)) with v=0v=0 on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y=d_\pm $$\end{document}y=d±. Genuine waves correspond to flows which depend on the variable (x-ct)(x-ct), while an incompressible shear flow is described by a velocity of the form (U(y), 0). Within the framework of linear theory (see [34, 36]), shear flow perturbations corresponding to the stream function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi (x,y,t)=\phi (y)\textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\alpha (x-ct)}$$\end{document}ψ(x,y,t)=ϕ(y)eiα(x-ct), where α >0α>0 is the wave-number in the x-direction and c is the complex wave speed, are governed by the Rayleigh-Kuo equation1.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (U-c) (\alpha ^2\phi -\phi '') - (\beta -U'')\phi =0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(U-c)(α2ϕ-ϕ′′)-(β-U′′)ϕ=0,with the boundary conditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi (d_-)=\phi (d_+)=0$$\end{document}ϕ(d-)=ϕ(d+)=0. Equation (1.2) has a singularity at a critical layer (a point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_c$$\end{document}yc with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(y_c)=c$$\end{document}U(yc)=c), since the coefficient of the highest-order derivative vanishes. In this case a perturbation analysis shows the appearance of vortices near the critical layer. In the absence of critical layers, instability can only arise if the imaginary part of the speed c, giving the growth rate of the perturbation, does not vanish. This leads to the condition that β -U''β-U′′ vanishes at some level as being necessary for linear instability (see [14]). In light of these classical results, we expect that genuine traveling waves can arise as coherent perturbations of a shear flow only under special circumstances. This perspective is supported by recent rigidity results showing that, under suitable conditions, certain shear flows do not admit any nontrivial traveling-wave perturbations [10, 20].

From the observational viewpoint, the four giant planets exhibit markedly different amounts of large-scale coherent wave activity. Several long-lived wave patterns have been observed on Jupiter and Saturn (some will be described below), but Uranus is the most featureless of the giant planets and Neptune’s most notable visible flow patterns are its dark spots. The very wide zonal bands on Neptune do not confine the dark spots tightly: for example, the largest, Neptune’s Great Dark Spot, first observed by Voyager 2 in 1989, drifted towards the equator and disappeared by 1994 (see [27]). On the other hand, on Jupiter and Saturn one finds a profusion of coherent waves: Saturn’s stationary hexagon, meandering ribbons, Jupiter’s chevrons and band-limited wave trains, as well as more intricate wave patterns surrounding the GRS.

The main aim of this paper is to provide a complete and rigorous classification of genuine traveling-wave solutions arising as coherent flow patterns with possibly large meridional velocity components and thus not necessarily representing small perturbations of a shear flow in a zonal band. In the context of Theorem 1.1, we denoted that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f_{\max }=\max _{(x,y)\in {\mathfrak {D}}_L}\{f(x,y)\}\quad \text {and}\quad f_{\min }=\min _{(x,y)\in {\mathfrak {D}}_L}\{f(x,y)\}\, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}fmax=max(x,y)∈DL{f(x,y)}andfmin=min(x,y)∈DL{f(x,y)}for a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in C({\mathfrak {D}}_L)$$\end{document}f∈C(DL). A genuine traveling wave is a periodic flow propagating in the East–West direction and presenting meridional variations, whereas flows with v ≡ 0v≡0 throughout \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {D}}_L$$\end{document}DL are termed shear flows – by the third equation in (1.3), shear flows depend only on the y-variable. The boundary condition (1.4) expresses the presence of zonal jets along it, confining the wave to the respective zonal band. Field data shows that the zonal jet systems of Jupiter and Saturn reach below the troposphere and have remained practically unchanged since the beginning of detailed spacecraft observations (see [17]). In addition to these zonal jets, some peculiar flow patterns occur: unlike the majority of wind jets on Jupiter and Saturn, near 47\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^\circ $$\end{document}∘N on Saturn, wavy perturbations of a jet produce a long-lived meandering ribbon (see Fig. 2) – a similar ribbon on Jupiter at 30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^\circ $$\end{document}∘N was rather short-lived. Let us also note that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Gamma =v_x-u_y+ \beta y \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Γ=vx-uy+βyis the total vorticity, comprising two distinct components: the relative vorticity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =v_x-u_y$$\end{document}γ=vx-uy due to the fluid motion and the planetary vorticity β yβy due solely to the planet’s rotation about its polar axis (this being the β β-plane correspondent of the concepts in spherical coordinates, discussed in [7]). By the third equation in (1.3), expressing mass conservation, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _y=\beta -\Delta u$$\end{document}Γy=β-Δu, which provides another perspective for the interpretation of generalized inflection points as stationary points of the variation of the total vorticity with latitude.Fig. 1Natural-color view of Saturn’s hexagon, taken with the Cassini spacecraft wide-angle camera on 22 July 2013 (credit: NASA/JPL-Caltech/Space Science Institute). In this paper, we provide, by means of Theorem 1.1, a complete classification of genuine traveling waves within the framework of the β β-plane approximation. Explicit mathematical examples of traveling waves for each class described in Theorem 1.1 are given in Examples 3.7-3.9, in addition to the observations described above and illustrated in Figs. 1-3. To prove Theorem 1.1, we first analyze in detail which values of c, located within the range of the zonal velocity, can arise as wave speeds of genuine traveling waves (see Theorem 3.1). By combining this analysis with the case when c lies outside Ran(u), we then establish the full classification of wave speeds, thereby proving Theorem 1.1. For a genuine traveling wave (u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y)), the values of c∈ Ran (u)c∈Ran(u) are of the following three types: a generalized inflection value of u, or a critical value of u, or a maximum/minimum of u if β >0β>0, while for β =0β=0 the values of c∈ Ran (u)c∈Ran(u) can only be generalized inflection values of u. The proof for β >0β>0 proceeds by contradiction and consists of two main parts. In the first part, we use the F-formulation (see Lemma 3.4) introduced in [10] and the sign of β β to demonstrate that v=0v=0 on the side \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u<c\}$$\end{document}{u<c} of the level set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u=c\}$$\end{document}{u=c}. This also enables us to show that the level set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u=c\}$$\end{document}{u=c} consists of several straight segments parallel to the zonal direction. In the second part, we show that v=0v=0 on the side \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u>c\}$$\end{document}{u>c} of the level set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u=c\}$$\end{document}{u=c}. We pursue this as a unique continuation problem for the 2-dimensional elliptic equation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta v+{\beta -\Delta u\over u-c}v=0$$\end{document}Δv+β-Δuu-cv=0. A natural approach is to reduce it to a 1-dimensional setting and to analyze it using classical ODE techniques, such as the Grönwall inequality or Fuchs-type methods. One attempt is to fix an arbitrary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0$$\end{document}x0 and to analyze the resulting ODE, but this generates an inhomogeneous term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v_{xx}(x_0,\cdot )$$\end{document}vxx(x0,·) that is difficult to control. Alternatively, one can exploit the periodicity in x and examine the equation mode by mode in Fourier space. In this framework, the nonlinear potential term induces intricate interactions among different modes, yielding a strongly coupled ODE system that is challenging to handle. Consequently, we abandon the 1-dimensional reduction and instead adopt a 2-dimensional approach. We employ the Carleman estimate (3.3)-(3.4) adapted to a boundary-aligned ball (see Fig. 6) around the level set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u=c\}$$\end{document}{u=c} to handle the Laplacian term. This localized estimate provides the necessary weighted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 bounds for w and ∇ w∇w, allowing the lower-order terms to be absorbed by the Laplacian term and yielding a quantitative unique continuation inequality in the ball, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w\in C^2$$\end{document}w∈C2 vanishes both near the center and outside the ball. A primary obstacle arises from the singular potential term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\beta -\Delta u\over u-c}$$\end{document}β-Δuu-c, which behaves like \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${1\over y-y_{i_0}}\notin L^{>1}$$\end{document}1y-yi0∉L>1 near a branch \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{y=y_{i_0}\}$$\end{document}{y=yi0} of the level set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u=c\}$$\end{document}{u=c}, so that the unique continuation results available in the research literature [3, 29, 44] cannot be invoked in this context. The key observation is that the vanishing of the meridional velocity v on the level set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u=c\}$$\end{document}{u=c} effectively weakens this singularity, enabling the application of the Hardy inequality in the normal direction. This in turn allows the weighted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 contribution of the singular potential to be controlled by that of the lower-order terms. Combined with the Carleman estimate, this provides us with the mechanism that ultimately establishes the quantitative unique continuation result across \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{y=y_{i_0}\}$$\end{document}{y=yi0}. We point out that our method establishes a 2-dimensional unique continuation mechanism for elliptic equations with a borderline \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${1\over y-y_{i_0}}$$\end{document}1y-yi0-type potential, when the solution vanishes on the singular set.

Note that Fig. 4 may alternatively be viewed as illustrating a rigidity phenomenon: namely, for a traveling wave \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))\in C^2({\mathfrak D}_L)$$\end{document}(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))∈C2(DL), if the wave speed c is lying in the black intervals, then it must be a shear flow. This gives rise to the natural question whether there are sufficient conditions to ensure that a traveling wave with wave speed in the red and blue intervals of Fig. 4 must be a shear flow. This question is challenging due to the singularity in the term 1øver u-c1u-c when the wave speed c lies inside the range of zonal velocity u. We present some sufficient conditions to ensure that: (i) a traveling wave with wave speed in the red intervals of Fig. 4 (i.e. in the range of the zonal velocity) must be a shear flow (see Theorem 3.10); (ii) a traveling wave with wave speed in the blue and red intervals of Fig. 4 (i.e. with arbitrary wave speed) must be a shear flow (see Theorem 3.12). Note that no restrictions are imposed on the amplitudes of the traveling waves. The sufficient condition in Theorem 3.12 is a narrow range of β β, and the proof makes full use of the F-formulation introduced in [10] combined with structural properties of the quasi-geostrophic equation. In particular, the argument establishing v =0v=0 on the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u > c\}$$\end{document}{u>c} differs from that in Theorem 3.1, where the analysis is simplified by exploiting the sign of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta - \Delta u$$\end{document}β-Δu. We also extend some of the classification and rigidity results for traveling waves to an unbounded channel in Theorems 4.1, 4.3 and 4.5.

We also investigate whether the rigidity of traveling waves (that is, the absence of genuine traveling waves) holds, or if genuine traveling waves exist near some classes of shear flows. The clarification of this issue plays an important role in the study of long time dynamics near a shear flow [13, 33, 35]. On the one hand, the rigidity of nearby traveling waves is a necessary condition for nonlinear inviscid damping of the shear flow and thus is a first step towards the asymptotic stability. On the other hand, if genuine nearby traveling waves do exist, then the long time dynamics near the shear flow is richer, as these waves might be ‘end states’ of nearby evolutionary flows.

We study the rigidity issue for monotone shear flows in Sect. 5. In an unbounded channel, as an application of Theorem 4.3, we give a simple description of the wave speeds of traveling waves near a monotone shear flow, from which, under the Rayleigh stability condition, we infer the absence of genuine nearby traveling waves (see Corollary 5.1 and Remark 5.2). In a bounded channel, unlike the unbounded case, the situation is more complicated. Under the Rayleigh stability condition, we fully characterize the parameter regimes for (β ,L)(β,L) that lead to the rigidity of traveling waves for arbitrary wave speeds versus those that admit genuine unidirectional traveling waves which are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2-close to the monotone shear flow. When the Rayleigh stability condition does not hold, we determine a parameter regime for (β ,L)(β,L) that admits genuine unidirectional nearby traveling waves. Outside this regime, the wave speed of any genuine nearby traveling wave must be a generalized inflection value (see Theorem 5.10). To prove this for a wave speed locating outside the blue interval in Fig. 4, we can apply Theorems 3.3 and 3.10. However, in general, it is hard to determine whether the rigidity of traveling waves with wave speeds in the blue interval holds or not. Let us point out that our approach for the rigidity part in Theorem 5.10 differs from that in [50], where the method depended heavily on the uniform \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^4$$\end{document}H4-bound of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 normalized meridional velocity, which requires higher regularity. We avoid studying the limit of a sequence of traveling waves, paying instead attention to the intrinsic structure of the nonlinear equation for a specific traveling wave and taking into account the spectral properties of the linearized operator associated with the β β-plane equation around the monotone shear flow. Basically, the spectral properties provide us a control from below for the linear part by a uniform \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1$$\end{document}H1 bound of the meridional velocity, while the nonlinear part turns out to provide a control from above by a smaller \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1$$\end{document}H1 bound, which is an impossible set-up. For the existence part in Theorem 5.10, we can not directly apply the bifurcation lemma established in Lemma 2.5 of [33], since the shear-flow profile lacks sufficient regularity. Our approach is to perturb it to a nearby shear flow with higher regularity. An issue that arises is that it is not clear whether the spectral condition in [33] is satisfied for the perturbed shear flow. To address this, we establish in Lemma 5.6 the continuous dependence of the principal eigenvalue of the singular Rayleigh-Kuo boundary value problem on the shear-flow profile in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2.

In contrast to the unbounded channel, where rigidity (for arbitrary c) holds for all (β , L)(β,L) under the Rayleigh stability condition, the bounded channel admits nearby genuine traveling waves for (β , L)(β,L) lying in a critical parameter regime. For (β , L)(β,L) in this critical parameter regime, we further prove that the monotone shear flow is nonlinearly Lyapunov stable. Nevertheless, the nearby coherent traveling waves act as nontrivial asymptotic states so that the shear flow is asymptotically unstable (see Theorem 5.19). This can be viewed as a subtle dynamical phenomenon arising from the inclusion of the Coriolis force, which is a hallmark of geophysical effects. The mechanism leading to asymptotic instability is the appearance of genuine traveling waves with wave speeds outside the zonal velocity, a situation that can never occur when the Coriolis effect is neglected.

Furthermore, by applying Theorem 3.12, we obtain a rigidity result for traveling waves with arbitrary wave speeds near a class of shear flows in Proposition 6.1, and then apply this result to the Couette–Poiseuille flow and the Bickley jet for a certain β β-range (see Corollaries 6.3 and 6.5). In the f-plane setting, as an application of the classification theorem, we also prove the rigidity of traveling waves with c≠ 0c≠0 near a Kolmogorov flow, and, in contrast, we construct nearby non-sheared steady flows in Proposition 6.11.

The results of this paper complement and improve some recent findings, specific details being provided in remarks throughout the paper. For the 2-dimensional incompressible Euler equation in a bounded periodic channel, the rigidity of steady flows with no horizontal stagnation points is proved in [31]. The sufficient condition to ensure the rigidity of steady flows is weakened to flows with no stagnation points in [20] and with the laminar property in [15]. On the other hand, near the Couette flow, the rigidity of traveling waves with arbitrary speeds is proved in (velocity) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{5\over 2}$$\end{document}H52 [35]. Near the Poiseuille flow, the rigidity of traveling waves with arbitrary speeds is proved in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{>6}$$\end{document}H>6 [13], and the rigidity of steady flows is proved in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2 [15]. See also [11, 15, 16, 18, 21, 22, 49] for other rigidity results.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Sect. 2 we recall the governing equations in vorticity form. Section 3 contains the core classification results: we first analyze which values of c lying in Ran(u) can serve as wave speeds of genuine traveling waves (Theorem 3.1), and then combine this with the case c∉ Ran (u)c∉Ran(u) to obtain the full classification of wave speeds (Theorem 1.1). We also discuss sufficient conditions for the rigidity of traveling waves with arbitrary amplitudes. Section 4 extends some of the classification and rigidity results to unbounded channels. In Sect. 5 we fully characterize the parameter regimes for (β , L)(β,L) that yield the following dynamics near a monotone shear flow: (i) the absence of genuine traveling waves; (ii) the existence of genuine traveling waves; and (iii) the constraint that wave speed of any possible genuine traveling wave must be a generalized inflection value. Section 6 is devoted to some further applications to specific non-monotone profiles.

The Governing Equations

It is convenient to write the β β-plane equation (1.3) in the form2.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _{t}\vec {u}+(\vec {u}\cdot \nabla )\vec {u}=-\nabla P-\beta yJ\vec {u}\,,\qquad \nabla \cdot \vec {u}=0 \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tu→+(u→·∇)u→=-∇P-βyJu→,∇·u→=0,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \ge 0$$\end{document}β≥0 is the Coriolis parameter, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vec {u}=(u,v)$$\end{document}u→=(u,v) is the fluid velocity, P is the modified pressure, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ J= \begin{pmatrix} 0 & -1\\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix} $$\end{document}J=0-110 is the rotation matrix. (f_0+\beta y_0)\psi $$\end{document}P=P-(f0+βy0)ψ, taking advantage of the existence of a stream function ψ ψ with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (u,v)=(-\partial _y\psi ,\partial _x\psi ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(u,v)=(-∂yψ,∂xψ),ensured by the incompressibility condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla \cdot \vec {u}=0$$\end{document}∇·u→=0.

The vorticity form of (2.1) is the 2-dimensional quasi-geostrophic vorticity equation2.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _{t}\gamma +(\vec {u}\cdot \nabla )\gamma +\beta v=\partial _{t}\gamma +\{\psi ,\gamma \}+\beta v =0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tγ+(u→·∇)γ+βv=∂tγ+{ψ,γ}+βv=0,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\psi ,\gamma \}=\partial _x\psi \partial _y\gamma -\partial _y\psi \partial _x\gamma $$\end{document}{ψ,γ}=∂xψ∂yγ-∂yψ∂xγ is the Poisson bracket, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma =\partial _{x}v-\partial _{y}u=\Delta \psi $$\end{document}γ=∂xv-∂yu=Δψ is the vorticity of the flow. Alternatively, we can write (2.3) in the form2.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _{t}\Gamma +(\vec {u}\cdot \nabla )\Gamma =0\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tΓ+(u→·∇)Γ=0,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma =\gamma + \beta y$$\end{document}Γ=γ+βy is the total vorticity. Note that (2.4) expresses the conservation of total vorticity along a fluid trajectory.

Classification and Rigidity of Traveling Waves

In this section, we present a comprehensive classification of the genuine traveling-wave solutions of the β β-plane equation. Subsequently, without imposing any smallness constraint on the amplitude, we derive some sufficient conditions ensuring the rigidity of traveling waves with wave speeds lying in the red and blue intervals depicted in Fig. 4.

Classification of Genuine Traveling Waves

We first investigate which values within the range of the zonal velocity are admissible wave speeds for genuine traveling waves.

Theorem 3.1

To prove Theorem 3.1 and Theorem 3.3, we employ the F-formulation of the β β-plane equation introduced in [10].

Lemma 3.4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))\in C^2(D_L)$$\end{document}(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))∈C2(DL) be a traveling-wave solution to the β β-plane equation (2.1)-(2.2). We will also use the following localized Carleman estimates in the unique-continuation argument for the proof of Theorem 3.1:

Lemma 3.5

(Carleman estimates) For any b>0b>0 and any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w\in C^2(\mathbb {T}_L\times [-d,d])$$\end{document}w∈C2(TL×[-d,d]) vanishing outside \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{\varepsilon _0}$$\end{document}Bε0 and on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{\varepsilon _0\over 2}$$\end{document}Bε02, we have3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{B_{\varepsilon _0}}r^{-2b-2}e^{2r^{-b}}|w|^2dxdy\le&{1\over 2b^4}\int _{B_{\varepsilon _0}}r^{b+2}e^{2r^{-b}}|\Delta w|^2dxdy, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Bε0r-2b-2e2r-b|w|2dxdy≤12b4∫Bε0rb+2e2r-b|Δw|2dxdy,and3.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{B_{\varepsilon _0}}e^{2r^{-b}}|\nabla w|^2dxdy\le&\left( {1\over 4b^4}+{1\over 2}\varepsilon _0^b+{2\over b^2}\right) \int _{B_{\varepsilon _0}}r^{b+2}e^{2r^{-b}}|\Delta w|^2dxdy, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Bε0e2r-b|∇w|2dxdy≤14b4+12ε0b+2b2∫Bε0rb+2e2r-b|Δw|2dxdy,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{\varepsilon _0}=B_{\varepsilon _0}(0,0),0<\varepsilon _0< \min \{1,\frac{L}{2}, d\}$$\end{document}Bε0=Bε0(0,0),0<ε0<min{1,L2,d} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r=\sqrt{x^2+y^2}$$\end{document}r=x2+y2.

The above Carleman estimates (3.3)-(3.4) provide weighted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 control of w and ∇ w∇w in terms of the weighted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 norm of Δ wΔw, and the coefficients on the right-hand side of (3.3)-(3.4) can be made arbitrarily small by choosing b>0b>0 sufficiently large. Moreover, the Carleman weight satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{r^{-b}}\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}e-b→∞ as r→ 0r→0, and thus enforces a strong amplification of any nonvanishing solution for b>0b>0 sufficiently large. These ingredients are essential for handling the Laplacian contribution in the quantitative unique continuation argument.

The condition that w vanishes on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{\varepsilon _0\over 2}$$\end{document}Bε02 guarantees the existence of the integrals in (3.3)-(3.4), while the condition that w vanishes outside \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{\varepsilon _0}$$\end{document}Bε0 ensures that the boundary terms in the integration by parts below vanish. The Carleman estimates (3.3)-(3.4) were derived in [40], a slight difference here being that b can be chosen as an arbitrary positive number (while in [40], b is required to be sufficiently large). For completeness we provide the proof.

Proof

Now, we are in a position to prove Theorem 3.1.

Proof of Theorem 3.1

The idea of the proof for β >0β>0 is as follows. On the side \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u<c\}$$\end{document}{u<c} of the level set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u=c\}$$\end{document}{u=c}, we make use of the F-formulation of the governing equation in Lemma 3.4 and take advantage of the sign of β β to show that v=0v=0. On the other side \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u>c\}$$\end{document}{u>c}, we employ a boundary-adapted Carleman estimate combined with a Hardy inequality to establish unique continuation for an elliptic equation with a borderline \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${1\over y-y_{i}}$$\end{document}1y-yi-type potential near a branch \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{y=y_{i}\}$$\end{document}{y=yi} of the level set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{u=c\}$$\end{document}{u=c}. Remark 3.6

We provide a supplement to Theorem 3.1 (i). For a traveling-wave solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))\in C^2(D_L)$$\end{document}(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))∈C2(DL) to the β β-plane equation (2.1)-(2.2), if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta -\Delta u\ne 0$$\end{document}β-Δu≠0 whenever u=cu=c, β >0β>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c=u_{\max }$$\end{document}c=umax, then it is a shear flow.

In fact, in the case β >0β>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c=u_{\max }$$\end{document}c=umax, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^+=\emptyset $$\end{document}S+=∅ and v=0v=0 on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^0$$\end{document}S0 due to (3.8) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta -\Delta u\ne 0$$\end{document}β-Δu≠0 whenever u=cu=c. Moreover, by (3.16) we have v=0v=0 on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^-$$\end{document}S-. Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^{\pm }$$\end{document}S± and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^0$$\end{document}S0 are defined in (3.9).

Proof of Lemma 3.2

For the parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\in (-\infty , u_{\min })$$\end{document}a∈(-∞,umin), the elliptic boundary value problem3.28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} -\partial _{x}((u-a)^2\partial _{x}G)-\partial _{y}((u-a)^2\partial _{y}G)=\Lambda (u-a)G,\quad G\in H_0^1(D_L)\cap H^2(D_L) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-∂x((u-a)2∂xG)-∂y((u-a)2∂yG)=Λ(u-a)G,G∈H01(DL)∩H2(DL)has infinitely countable eigenvalues \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\Lambda _n(a)\}_{n=1}^\infty $$\end{document}{Λn(a)}n=1∞, which are all real and can be arranged in the non-decreasing order: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _n(a)\le \Lambda _{n+1}(a)$$\end{document}Λn(a)≤Λn+1(a) for any integer n≥ 1n≥1, counting repeatedly according to their multiplicity. Moreover, the n-th eigenvalue \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _n(a)$$\end{document}Λn(a) has the variational expression\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Lambda _{n}(a)=\sup _{V\subset H_0^1({ D}_L),\dim V=n-1}\,\inf _{G\perp V, G\in H_0^1({ D}_L), G\ne 0}{\int _{{D}_L}(u-a)^2|\nabla G|^2dxdy\over \int _{{ D}_L} (u-a)G^2dxdy}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Λn(a)=supV⊂H01(DL),dimV=n-1infG⊥V,G∈H01(DL),G≠0∫DL(u-a)2|∇G|2dxdy∫DL(u-a)G2dxdy.For an integer n≥ 1n≥1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\Lambda _n(a), a\in (-\infty , u_{\min })\}$$\end{document}{Λn(a),a∈(-∞,umin)} forms an eigenvalue curve.

Since c∉ Ran(u)c∉Ran(u), by Theorems 3.2 and 3.11 of [10] we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in [c_\beta ^+,u_{\min })$$\end{document}c∈[cβ+,umin). By Lemma 3.4, the function F defined by (3.1) solves (3.28) with a=ca=c and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda =\beta $$\end{document}Λ=β on the whole domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_L$$\end{document}DL. This implies that β β is an eigenvalue of the elliptic boundary value problem (3.28) with a=ca=c. Then there exists some integer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_0\ge 1$$\end{document}n0≥1 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _{n_0}(a)=\beta $$\end{document}Λn0(a)=β holds for a=ca=c. □ □

Proof of Theorem 3.3

Let β >0β>0. Since (u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y)) is a genuine traveling wave, by Theorems 3.2 and 3.11 in [10] we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in [c^{+}_{\beta },u_{\max }]$$\end{document}c∈[cβ+,umax]. If c is outside Ran(u), then by Lemma 3.2 we know that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in [c_\beta ^+,u_{\min })$$\end{document}c∈[cβ+,umin) solves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda _{n_0}(c)=\beta $$\end{document}Λn0(c)=β for some integer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_0\ge 1$$\end{document}n0≥1. Next, we consider c∈ Ran(u)c∈Ran(u). If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in (u_{\min }, u_{\max })$$\end{document}c∈(umin,umax), by Theorem 3.1 (i), we know that c is either a generalized inflection value of u or a critical value of u.

For β =0β=0, since (u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y)) is a genuine traveling wave, by Theorem 1.1 in [20] (or Theorem 1.1 in [31]) we know that c∈ Ran(u)c∈Ran(u). By Theorem 3.1 (ii), c must be a generalized inflection value of u. □ □

We now provide mathematical examples to demonstrate that genuine traveling waves in each of the classification types described in Theorem 1.1 do exist.

Rigidity of Traveling Waves with Arbitrary Amplitudes

We first establish a rigidity result for traveling waves with wave speeds lying in the red intervals of Fig. 4. The conclusions differ between the f-plane and β β-plane settings.

Theorem 3.10

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \notin Ran(\Delta u)$$\end{document}β∉Ran(Δu) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))\in C^2(D_L)$$\end{document}(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))∈C2(DL) be a traveling-wave solution to the β β-plane equation (2.1)-(2.2). Assume that one of the following conditions (i)β >0β>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\notin [c_\beta ^+, u_{\min }]$$\end{document}c∉[cβ+,umin] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla u\ne 0$$\end{document}∇u≠0 on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_L$$\end{document}DL,(ii)β =0β=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}c∈R holds.Then (u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y)) is a shear flow, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_\beta ^{+}$$\end{document}cβ+ is defined in (1.5) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\pm }=\pm d$$\end{document}d±=±d.

(i)β >0β>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\notin [c_\beta ^+, u_{\min }]$$\end{document}c∉[cβ+,umin] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla u\ne 0$$\end{document}∇u≠0 on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_L$$\end{document}DL,

In general, it is hard to determine whether the rigidity of traveling waves with speeds in the blue interval holds or not. Remark 3.13

In a bounded periodic channel, Theorem 3.12 offers a generalization of Corollary \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1.6\, (\textrm{i})$$\end{document}1.6(i) in [18] through a different approach, improving it in two aspects: one extension is from the f-plane to the β β-plane setting, and the other is that the amplitudes of the traveling waves are arbitrary, with no smallness constraint. In Subsection 6.1 we present its application to the rigidity of traveling waves near a class of shear flows which include the Couette–Poiseuille flow and the Bickley jet.

Results for An Unbounded Channel

We study the fluid flow in the unbounded channel\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} D_L^{\infty }=\{(x,y): x\in \mathbb {T}_L,\,y\in (-\infty ,+\infty )\}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}DL∞={(x,y):x∈TL,y∈(-∞,+∞)},with the asymptotic behavior4.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} v\rightarrow 0\quad \text {as}\quad y\rightarrow \pm \infty \end{aligned}$$\end{document}v→0asy→±∞for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {T}_L$$\end{document}x∈TL. Denote that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F_{\sup }=\sup _{(x,y)\in D_L^{\infty }}\{F(x,y)\}\,,\quad F_{\inf }=\inf _{(x,y)\in D_L^{\infty }}\{F(x,y)\}\, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Fsup=sup(x,y)∈DL∞{F(x,y)},Finf=inf(x,y)∈DL∞{F(x,y)}for a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \in C(D_L^{\infty })$$\end{document}F∈C(DL∞).

We study the propagation speeds and structural properties of traveling waves, extending prior results to an unbounded channel. First, we extend Theorems 3.2 and 3.11 in [10] to an unbounded channel, thus addressing the rigidity issue for wave speeds located outside the range of the zonal velocity.

Theorem 4.1

(i)β >0β>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c>u_{\sup }$$\end{document}c>usup;

(ii)β =0β=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\notin \overline{Ran(u)}$$\end{document}c∉Ran(u)¯holds. Then (u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y)) is a shear flow.

β =0β=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\notin \overline{Ran(u)}$$\end{document}c∉Ran(u)¯

holds. Then (u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y)) is a shear flow.

Remark 4.2

The condition “\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\partial _yv\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}v∂yv→0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v^2\partial _y u\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}v2∂yu→0 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\rightarrow \pm \infty $$\end{document}y→±∞ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {T}_L$$\end{document}x∈TL" is mild. In fact, by (4.1) and the incompressibility condition, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u\in \dot{W}^{1,\infty }(D_L^{\infty })=\{u:\partial _xu,\partial _y u\in L^\infty (D_L^\infty )\}$$\end{document}u∈W˙1,∞(DL∞)={u:∂xu,∂yu∈L∞(DL∞)} is sufficient to guarantee this.

Note that in the unbounded case, we do not obtain a lower bound for the wave speeds of genuine traveling waves in the β β-plane setting. This is because the unbounded domain prevents the Poincaré inequality (see (3.29) in [10]) from being carried out. This can also be seen from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_\beta ^+\rightarrow -\infty $$\end{document}cβ+→-∞ as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{\pm }\rightarrow \pm \infty $$\end{document}d±→±∞, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_\beta ^+$$\end{document}cβ+ is defined in (1.5).

We now extend the classification of the traveling waves to an unbounded channel. We do not intend to get the generalized version as in Theorem 3.3, but focus on some special case, which is applicable to the traveling waves near monotone shear flows in the next section.

Theorem 4.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))\in C^2(D_L^{\infty })$$\end{document}(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))∈C2(DL∞) be a genuine traveling-wave solution to the β β-plane equation (2.1), subject to (4.1). Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\partial _{y}u|\ge C_0$$\end{document}|∂yu|≥C0 on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_L^{\infty }$$\end{document}DL∞ for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0>0$$\end{document}C0>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in \dot{W}^{1,\infty }(D_L^{\infty })$$\end{document}v∈W˙1,∞(DL∞), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${v^2\partial _y u\over u}\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}v2∂yuu→0 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\rightarrow \pm \infty $$\end{document}y→±∞ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {T}_L$$\end{document}x∈TL. Remark 4.4

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textrm{i})$$\end{document}(i) Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim _{y\rightarrow \pm \infty }|u(x,y)|=\infty $$\end{document}limy→±∞|u(x,y)|=∞ and thus Ran(u(x,· ))=Ran(u)Ran(u(x,·))=Ran(u) spans \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {T}_L$$\end{document}x∈TL in Theorem 4.3, a situation that differs from the case of a bounded channel (for which Ran(u) is compact).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm{(ii)}$$\end{document}(ii) By (4.1), a sufficient condition for the asymptotic behavior “\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${v^2\partial _y u\over u}\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}v2∂yuu→0 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\rightarrow \pm \infty $$\end{document}y→±∞ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {T}_L$$\end{document}x∈TL" is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\partial _{y}u\over u}$$\end{document}∂yuu is bounded for |y| sufficiently large.

Proof

The proof essentially follows the same approach as in Theorem 3.1. We highlight only a difference, primarily concerning the asymptotic behavior as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\rightarrow \pm \infty $$\end{document}y→±∞.

Finally, we extend Theorem 3.12 to the unbounded channel, providing sufficient conditions for the rigidity of traveling waves with arbitrary wave speeds.

Theorem 4.5

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))\in C^2(D_L^{\infty })$$\end{document}(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))∈C2(DL∞) be a traveling-wave solution to the β β-plane equation (2.1), subject to (4.1). \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in \dot{W}^{2,\infty }(D_L^{\infty })=\{v:\partial _xv,\partial _y v, \Delta v\in L^\infty (D_L^\infty )\}$$\end{document}v∈W˙2,∞(DL∞)={v:∂xv,∂yv,Δv∈L∞(DL∞)}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} Applications to Monotone Shear Flows

In this section, based on the theoretical framework developed in Sects. 3-4, we present a precise classification of the traveling waves near a monotone shear flow. In an unbounded channel, we get a simple description of the propagation speeds of nearby traveling waves in Corollary 5.1. In the bounded channel setting, the situation becomes complicated and we present a comprehensive analysis of nearby traveling waves. First, we obtain the rigidity of nearby traveling waves with wave speeds lying inside the range of the monotone shear flow. Under the Rayleigh stability condition, we provide a complete characterization of the (β ,L)(β,L)-parameter regimes that result in either the rigidity of traveling waves for arbitrary wave speeds, or the emergence of genuine unidirectional traveling waves that are close to the monotone shear flow. In the absence of Rayleigh stability condition, we identify a parameter regime for (β ,L)(β,L) that allows for the existence of genuine unidirectional traveling waves in a neighborhood of the monotone shear flow. Outside this parameter regime, the wave speed of any genuine nearby traveling wave must be a generalized inflection value of the zonal velocity, see Theorem 5.10 and Remark 5.11. Our results hold for general monotone shear flows and in the special case of the Couette flow we improve Theorem 1.1 and Theorem 1.3 in [50]; see Remark 5.4 and Corollary 5.15.

Under the Rayleigh stability condition, while monotone shear flows are isolated in an unbounded channel, in a bounded channel genuine traveling waves arise near such shear flows for a large (β , L)(β,L)-parameter regime. Moreover, for (β ,L)(β,L) in this regime, the monotone shear flow is nonlinearly Lyapunov stable yet asymptotically unstable.

Monotone Shear Flows in An Unbounded Channel

We consider monotone shear flows \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_0,0)$$\end{document}(u0,0) satisfying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|u_0'|\ge C_0$$\end{document}|u0′|≥C0 on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0>0$$\end{document}C0>0, a typical example being the Couette flow. A characteristic of such shear flows is that the range of zonal velocity spans the entire real line, which allows us to deal with arbitrary wave speeds.

Corollary 5.1
