This shows that for a general monotone shear flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_0,0)$$\end{document}(u0,0) in an unbounded channel, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \notin \overline{Ran(u_0'')}$$\end{document}β∉Ran(u0′′)¯, genuine nearby traveling waves are absent. This is a necessary condition for asymptotic stability (i.e. nonlinear inviscid damping) near a shear flow in a specific space. Taking for example the Couette flow, it is shown in [4] that nonlinear inviscid damping near it holds in a certain Gevrey space for β =0β=0. This result was extended to the case β >0β>0 in [50]. Applying the above consequence to the Couette flow, we conclude that there are no genuine traveling waves \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{2,\infty }(D_L^{\infty })$$\end{document}W2,∞(DL∞)-close (in velocity) to (y, 0) for β >0β>0. This is consistent with Theorem 1.7 in [50].

Monotone Shear Flows in a Bounded Channel

We first establish a rigidity result for traveling waves near a monotone shear flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_0,0)$$\end{document}(u0,0) with wave speeds inside \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Ran (u_0)$$\end{document}Ran(u0).

Corollary 5.3

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0\in C^2([-d,d])$$\end{document}u0∈C2([-d,d]) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta \in (0,{u_{0,\max }-u_{0,\min }\over 2})$$\end{document}δ∈(0,u0,max-u0,min2). Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|u_0'|\ge C_0$$\end{document}|u0′|≥C0 on [-d,d][-d,d] for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0>0$$\end{document}C0>0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \notin Ran(u_0'')$$\end{document}β∉Ran(u0′′), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in [u_{0,\min }+\delta ,u_{0,\max }-\delta ]$$\end{document}c∈[u0,min+δ,u0,max-δ]. Let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0<\varepsilon _\delta <\min \{\delta ,C_0,|(u_0'')_{\min }-\beta |,|(u_0'')_{\max }-\beta |\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0<εδ<min{δ,C0,|(u0′′)min-β|,|(u0′′)max-β|}.Then any traveling-wave solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))\in C^2(D_L)$$\end{document}(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))∈C2(DL) to the β β-plane equation (2.1)-(2.2) satisfying5.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert (u,v)-(u_{0},0)\Vert _{C^{2}(D_L)}<\varepsilon _\delta \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖(u,v)-(u0,0)‖C2(DL)<εδmust be a shear flow.

Proof

This is a consequence of Theorem 3.1. Indeed, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \notin Ran(u_0'')$$\end{document}β∉Ran(u0′′), by (5.3) and the choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _\delta $$\end{document}εδ we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \notin Ran(\Delta u)$$\end{document}β∉Ran(Δu). Moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |\partial _{y}u|\ge C_0-\varepsilon _\delta >0$$\end{document}|∂yu|≥C0-εδ>0 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,y)\in D_L. $$\end{document}(x,y)∈DL. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x_1,y_1),(x_2,y_2)\in D_L$$\end{document}(x1,y1),(x2,y2)∈DL such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_{0}(x_1,y_1)=u_{0,\min }\quad \text {and}\quad u_{0}(x_2,y_2)=u_{0,\max }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u0(x1,y1)=u0,minandu0(x2,y2)=u0,max.Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in [u_{0,\min }+\delta ,u_{0,\max }-\delta ]$$\end{document}c∈[u0,min+δ,u0,max-δ], by (5.3) we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} c-u_{\min }\ge c-u(x_1,y_1)= c-u_0(x_1,y_1)+u_0(x_1,y_1)-u(x_1,y_1)\ge \delta -\varepsilon _\delta >0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}c-umin≥c-u(x1,y1)=c-u0(x1,y1)+u0(x1,y1)-u(x1,y1)≥δ-εδ>0,and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} c-u_{\max }\le c-u(x_2,y_2)= c-u_0(x_2,y_2)+u_0(x_2,y_2)-u(x_2,y_2)\le -\delta +\varepsilon _\delta <0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}c-umax≤c-u(x2,y2)=c-u0(x2,y2)+u0(x2,y2)-u(x2,y2)≤-δ+εδ<0.Thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in (u_{\min },u_{\max })$$\end{document}c∈(umin,umax) and all assumptions in Theorem 3.1 hold for (u, v). Therefore (u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y)) is a shear flow. □ □

Remark 5.4

Corollary 5.3 extends Theorem 1.1 in [50] from the Couette flow setting (in which case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Ran(u_0'')=\{0\}$$\end{document}Ran(u0′′)={0}) to general monotone shear flows, while simultaneously improving the regularity assumption from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{\ge 5}$$\end{document}H≥5 to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2.

Regarding Corollary 5.3, two natural questions arise: the first is whether there are genuine nearby traveling waves outside the range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[u_{0,\min }+\delta ,u_{0,\max }-\delta ]$$\end{document}[u0,min+δ,u0,max-δ] for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \notin Ran(u_0'')$$\end{document}β∉Ran(u0′′), the other regards specific features of traveling waves for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \in Ran(u_0'')$$\end{document}β∈Ran(u0′′). We will provide answers in Theorem 5.10. To proceed, we first discuss some relevant spectral aspects. Lemma 5.5

Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0\in C^2([-d,d])$$\end{document}u0∈C2([-d,d]) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0'\ne 0$$\end{document}u0′≠0 on [-d,d][-d,d]. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1(\cdot ,u_{0,\min })$$\end{document}λ1(·,u0,min) is continuous on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R.

Lemma 5.6

It is useful to determine the limits of the principal eigenvalue \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1(\beta ,c)$$\end{document}λ1(β,c) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\rightarrow -\infty $$\end{document}c→-∞ and as c approaches the left endpoint of the range of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0$$\end{document}u0.

Lemma 5.7

Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0\in C^2([-d,d])$$\end{document}u0∈C2([-d,d]) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0'\ne 0$$\end{document}u0′≠0 on [-d,d][-d,d]. Then5.19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{c\rightarrow u_{0,\min }^-}\lambda _1(\beta ,c)=\lambda _1(\beta ,u_{0,\min })\quad \text {and}\quad \lim _{c\rightarrow -\infty }\lambda _1(\beta ,c)={\pi ^2\over 4d^2}>0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limc→u0,min-λ1(β,c)=λ1(β,u0,min)andlimc→-∞λ1(β,c)=π24d2>0.

Proof

Since the second limit in (5.19) is straightforward, we only provide a proof for the first. Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0'>0$$\end{document}u0′>0 on [-d,d][-d,d]. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in (-\infty ,u_{0,\min }]$$\end{document}c∈(-∞,u0,min], we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_0(y)-c&=u_0(y)-u_{0,\min }+u_{0,\min }-c\ge u_0(y)-u_{0,\min }\\&= u_0(y)-u_{0}(-d)\ge C(y+d)\,,\quad y\in [-d,d]\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u0(y)-c=u0(y)-u0,min+u0,min-c≥u0(y)-u0,min=u0(y)-u0(-d)≥C(y+d),y∈[-d,d].It follows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left| {\beta - u_0''\over u_0-c}\right| \le {C\over y+d},\qquad y\in [-d,d], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}β-u0′′u0-c≤Cy+d,y∈[-d,d],where C>0C>0 is independent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in (-\infty ,u_{0,\min }]$$\end{document}c∈(-∞,u0,min]. By the same argument as in (5.17), we obtain the uniform eigenvalue-bound\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda _1(C,-d;y)\le \lambda _1(\beta ,c)\le \lambda _1(-C,-d;y),\qquad c\in (-\infty ,u_{0,\min }]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λ1(C,-d;y)≤λ1(β,c)≤λ1(-C,-d;y),c∈(-∞,u0,min].This, in turn, yields a uniform eigenfunction-bound\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \phi '_{c}\Vert _{L^2(-d,d)}&\le M_0,\qquad c\in (-\infty ,u_{0,\min }], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖ϕc′‖L2(-d,d)≤M0,c∈(-∞,u0,min],where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _c\in H_0^1(-d,d)$$\end{document}ϕc∈H01(-d,d) is an eigenfunction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1(\beta ,c)$$\end{document}λ1(β,c) normalized by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_0$$\end{document}M0 is defined in (5.18). Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0\longleftarrow&C(c-u_{0,\min })M_0^2\\ \le&C(c-u_{0,\min })\Vert \phi _{c}'\Vert _{L^2(-d,d)}^2\\ \le&C(c-u_{0,\min })\int _{-d}^d{1\over (y+d)^2}|\phi _{c}|^2 dy\\ \le&\int _{-d}^d\left( |\phi _{c}'|^2-{\beta -u_0''\over u_0-c}|\phi _{c}|^2\right) dy -\int _{-d}^d\left( |\phi _{c}'|^2-{\beta -u_0''\over u_0-u_{0,\min }}|\phi _{c}|^2\right) dy\\ \le&\lambda _1(\beta ,c)-\lambda _1(\beta ,u_{0,\min })\\ \le&\int _{-d}^d\left( |\phi _{u_{0,\min }}'|^2-{\beta -u_0''\over u_0-c}|\phi _{u_{0,\min }}|^2\right) dy\\&-\int _{-d}^d\left( |\phi _{u_{0,\min }}'|^2-{\beta -u_0''\over u_0-u_{0,\min }}|\phi _{u_{0,\min }}|^2\right) dy\\ =&\, (u_{0,\min }-c)\int _{-d}^d{\beta -u_0''\over (u_0-c)(u_0-u_{0,\min })}|\phi _{u_{0,\min }}|^2 dy\\ \le&C(u_{0,\min }-c)\int _{-d}^d{1\over (y+d)^2}|\phi _{u_{0,\min }}|^2 dy\\ \le&C(u_{0,\min }-c)\Vert \phi _{u_{0,\min }}'\Vert _{L^2(-d,d)}^2\longrightarrow 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0⟵C(c-u0,min)M02≤C(c-u0,min)‖ϕc′‖L2(-d,d)2≤C(c-u0,min)∫-dd1(y+d)2|ϕc|2dy≤∫-dd|ϕc′|2-β-u0′′u0-c|ϕc|2dy-∫-dd|ϕc′|2-β-u0′′u0-u0,min|ϕc|2dy≤λ1(β,c)-λ1(β,u0,min)≤∫-dd|ϕu0,min′|2-β-u0′′u0-c|ϕu0,min|2dy-∫-dd|ϕu0,min′|2-β-u0′′u0-u0,min|ϕu0,min|2dy=(u0,min-c)∫-ddβ-u0′′(u0-c)(u0-u0,min)|ϕu0,min|2dy≤C(u0,min-c)∫-dd1(y+d)2|ϕu0,min|2dy≤C(u0,min-c)‖ϕu0,min′‖L2(-d,d)2⟶0since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\rightarrow u_{0,\min }^-$$\end{document}c→u0,min-. This establishes the first limit in (5.19). □ □

Due to (5.5), we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1(\beta ,u_{0,\min })>0$$\end{document}λ1(β,u0,min)>0 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta <(u_0'')_{\min }$$\end{document}β<(u0′′)min. We now prove the positiveness of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1(0,u_{0,\min })$$\end{document}λ1(0,u0,min).

Lemma 5.8

Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0\in C^2([-d,d])$$\end{document}u0∈C2([-d,d]) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0'\ne 0$$\end{document}u0′≠0 on [-d,d][-d,d]. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1(0,u_{0,\min })>0$$\end{document}λ1(0,u0,min)>0, and consequently, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{-1}(0,u_{0,\min })>0$$\end{document}λ1-1(0,u0,min)>0.

Proof

Without loss of generality, we may assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0'\ge C_0$$\end{document}u0′≥C0 on [-d,d][-d,d] for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0>0$$\end{document}C0>0. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _0\in H_0^1(-d,d)$$\end{document}ϕ0∈H01(-d,d) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \phi _0\Vert _{L^2(-d,d)}=1$$\end{document}‖ϕ0‖L2(-d,d)=1 such that (5.12) holds with β =0β=0. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda _1(0,u_{0,\min })=&\, \int _{-d}^{d}\left( |\phi '_{0}|^2+{u_0''\over u_0-u_{0,\min }}|\phi _{0}|^2\right) dy\\ =&\, \int _{-d}^{d}|\phi '_{0}|^2dy+{u_0'\phi _0^2\over u_0-u_0(-d)}\bigg |_{y=-d}^d-\int _{-d}^du_0'\left( {\phi _0^2\over u_0-u_{0,\min }}\right) 'dy. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λ1(0,u0,min)=∫-dd|ϕ0′|2+u0′′u0-u0,min|ϕ0|2dy=∫-dd|ϕ0′|2dy+u0′ϕ02u0-u0(-d)|y=-dd-∫-ddu0′ϕ02u0-u0,min′dy.Since\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\left| {u_0'(y)\phi _0(y)^2\over u_0(y)-u_0(-d)}\right| \le {|u_0'(y)|\Vert \phi _0'\Vert _{L^2(-d,y)}^2(y+d)\over C_0(y+d)}\rightarrow 0\quad \text {as}\quad y\rightarrow -d, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u0′(y)ϕ0(y)2u0(y)-u0(-d)≤|u0′(y)|‖ϕ0′‖L2(-d,y)2(y+d)C0(y+d)→0asy→-d,and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\int _{-d}^d{\phi _0\phi _0'u_0'\over u_0-u_{0,\min }}dy\le C\Vert \phi _0'\Vert _{L^2(-d,d)}^2,\,\,\int _{-d}^d{\phi _0^2u_0'^2\over (u_0-u_{0,\min })^2}dy\le C\Vert \phi _0'\Vert _{L^2(-d,d)}^2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫-ddϕ0ϕ0′u0′u0-u0,mindy≤C‖ϕ0′‖L2(-d,d)2,∫-ddϕ02u0′2(u0-u0,min)2dy≤C‖ϕ0′‖L2(-d,d)2,we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda _1(0,u_{0,\min })=&\, \int _{-d}^{d}\left( |\phi '_{0}|^2-{2\phi _0\phi _0'u_0'\over u_0-u_{0,\min }}+{\phi _0^2u_0'^2\over (u_0-u_{0,\min })^2}\right) dy\\ =&\, \int _{-d}^{d}\left( \phi '_{0}-{\phi _0u_0'\over u_0-u_{0,\min }}\right) ^2dy\ge 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λ1(0,u0,min)=∫-dd|ϕ0′|2-2ϕ0ϕ0′u0′u0-u0,min+ϕ02u0′2(u0-u0,min)2dy=∫-ddϕ0′-ϕ0u0′u0-u0,min2dy≥0.Suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} Remark 5.9

In Lemma 5.8, if we replace the monotone shear flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0$$\end{document}u0 by a non-monotone shear flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0$$\end{document}u0 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0$$\end{document}u0 attains its minimum only at the endpoints ± d±d and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0'(\pm d)\ne 0$$\end{document}u0′(±d)≠0, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1(0,u_{0,\min })\ge 0$$\end{document}λ1(0,u0,min)≥0. Moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1(0,u_{0,\min })=0$$\end{document}λ1(0,u0,min)=0 can be achieved by considering the shear flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0(y)=\cos \left( {\pi \over 2} y\right) $$\end{document}u0(y)=cosπ2y on [-1,1][-1,1]. Indeed, it can be directly verified that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\cos \left( {\pi \over 2} y\right) $$\end{document}cosπ2y is an eigenfunction of the principal eigenvalue \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1(0,u_{0,\min })=0$$\end{document}λ1(0,u0,min)=0 for the eigenvalue problem (5.4).

Now we are ready to completely characterize traveling waves near a monotone shear flow in a bounded channel.

Remark 5.11

In Theorem 5.10 (iii), it is natural to ask whether there exists a monotone shear flow with genuine nearby traveling waves having wave speeds which are generalized inflection values of the zonal velocity. A concrete example is the monotone shear flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(U_{b,a},0)$$\end{document}(Ub,a,0) constructed in (5.6) of [50], which satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_{b,a}'>0$$\end{document}Ub,a′>0 on [-1,1][-1,1], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_{b,a}(0)=0$$\end{document}Ub,a(0)=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta =U_{b,a}''(0)$$\end{document}β=Ub,a′′(0), with b>0b>0 sufficiently small and a>0a>0. By Theorem 1.5 in [50], there exists a genuine traveling wave (u(x, y), v(x, y)) with zero wave speed (i.e. a steady flow) near the shear flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(U_{b,a},0)$$\end{document}(Ub,a,0) for some b, a>0b,a>0 sufficiently small. Since (u, v) is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2 close enough to the monotone shear flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(U_{b,a},0)$$\end{document}(Ub,a,0), c=0∈ Ran(u)c=0∈Ran(u) and c is neither a critical value nor a maximum/minimum of u. By Theorem 3.1, c=0c=0 must be a generalized inflection value of u.

Remark 5.13

Let us compare the rigidity property of traveling waves with arbitrary wave speeds near monotone shear flows in bounded versus unbounded channels. Assume that the Rayleigh stability condition (i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \notin \overline{Ran(u_0'')}$$\end{document}β∉Ran(u0′′)¯) holds. In an unbounded channel, the rigidity near general monotone shear flows with appropriate asymptotic behavior is proved for arbitrary zonal periods (see Remark 5.2). In a bounded channel, however, it depends on β β and on the zonal period L, see Theorem 5.10 and Remark 5.12. In particular, we provide critical regions of (β ,L)(β,L) such that the rigidity near monotone shear flows does not hold in Theorem 5.10, see the green regions of Fig. 7 for Case 2 in Remark 5.12.

Example 5.14

As an illustration of the physical relevance of Theorem 5.10, we consider Case 3 of Remark 5.12. The data provided in Fig. 12.5 of [42] shows the presence of steady waves (with zero speed) in the zonal band between 72\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^\circ $$\end{document}∘N and 78\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^\circ $$\end{document}∘N on Saturn, with an underlying shear flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0(y)$$\end{document}u0(y) that increases poleward and has a strictly concave shape. For this zonal band we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta>0>(u_0'')_{\max }$$\end{document}β>0>(u0′′)max, and Theorem 5.10 ensures that only waves with sufficiently large wavelengths can arise as perturbations of this shear flow. The wavelength of the observed waves, which form Saturn’s hexagon, is indeed about 14500 km (see [9]).

Applying Theorem 5.10 to the Couette flow (i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0(y)=y$$\end{document}u0(y)=y, y∈ [-1,1]y∈[-1,1]) and using the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{1}^{-1}(0,-1)>0$$\end{document}λ1-1(0,-1)>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Ran(u_0'')=\{0\}$$\end{document}Ran(u0′′)={0}, we get the following result.

Corollary 5.15

Remark 5.16

In the β β-plane setting, compared to Theorem 1.3 in [50], Corollary 5.15 improves the regularity requirement, reducing it from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{\ge 5}$$\end{document}H≥5 to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2. In the f-plane setting, we mention that the rigidity of traveling waves with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}c∈R holds for L>0L>0 near the Couette flow in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{>{5\over 2}}$$\end{document}H>52 [35].

Remark 5.17

The cases 1-3 in Remark 5.12 show that when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1((u_0'')_{\max },u_{0,\min })>0$$\end{document}λ1((u0′′)max,u0,min)>0, then the value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{-1}(0,u_{0,\min })$$\end{document}λ1-1(0,u0,min) serves as the transitional β β-parameter for the onset of genuine traveling waves near the monotone shear flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_0,0)$$\end{document}(u0,0) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2. More precisely, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \le \lambda _1^{-1}(0,u_{0,\min })$$\end{document}β≤λ1-1(0,u0,min), the rigidity of nearby traveling waves holds for all wave speeds and for all zonal periods, while if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta >\lambda _1^{-1}(0,u_{0,\min })$$\end{document}β>λ1-1(0,u0,min), there exist genuine nearby traveling waves for some large wavelengths.

We use Matlab to numerically compute the transitional β β-parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _1^{-1}(0,u_{0,\min })$$\end{document}λ1-1(0,u0,min) for monotone shear flows with either linear profiles or concave parabolic profiles. These computations illustrate how the transitional β β-value varies as the zonal-band width increases or as the background shear intensifies. To emphasize the dependence on both the shear profile and the zonal-band width, we rewrite the transitional parameter as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _\mathrm{{crit}}(u_0, d)=\lambda _1^{-1}(0,u_{0,\min })$$\end{document}βcrit(u0,d)=λ1-1(0,u0,min).

Concave parabolic profiles: For the concave parabolic profiles \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0(y)=-y^2+by+e$$\end{document}u0(y)=-y2+by+e confined to the zonal band [-d,d][-d,d], we impose the condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d<{b\over 2}$$\end{document}d<b2 to ensure that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0$$\end{document}u0 is monotone on this band. The corresponding Rayleigh-Kuo boundary value problem (5.4) is5.46\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} -\phi ''+{\beta +2\over (y+d)(y-d-b)}\phi =\lambda \phi , \,\,\,\,\phi (\pm d)=0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-ϕ′′+β+2(y+d)(y-d-b)ϕ=λϕ,ϕ(±d)=0.Note that in a fixed zonal band, increasing b displaces the parabola horizontally to the right while maintaining its shape, resulting in a stronger shear effect. Numerical computation of the eigenvalues of (5.46) yields the transitional β β-values listed in Table 2, which exhibit trends (i)-(ii) comparable to those observed for the linear profiles.

Example 5.18

We use the numerical computation of the transitional β β-value in Remark 5.17 to explain the lack of waves in Jupiter’s zonal band between 37\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^\circ $$\end{document}∘S and 39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^\circ $$\end{document}∘S that was noted in [39]. Indeed, the profile \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0$$\end{document}u0 of the zonal flow in this band is linearly sheared, featuring an eastward jet with speed 45 m/s along the northern boundary and a westward jet with speed 5 m/s along the southern boundary (see Fig. 4 in [26] for accurate data). By (1.1), a bandwidth of two degrees of latitude corresponds to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2d={\pi \over 90}\approx 0.035$$\end{document}2d=π90≈0.035. Using the values listed in Table 1 together with the dimensionless velocity expression (2.6) in [10], we obtain the boundary values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0(-d)=-{1\over 30}$$\end{document}u0(-d)=-130 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0(d)={3\over 10}$$\end{document}u0(d)=310, yielding the linear profile \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0(y)=ay+b$$\end{document}u0(y)=ay+b with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a={1\over 6d}$$\end{document}a=16d and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b={2\over 15}$$\end{document}b=215, which describes the observed flow pattern. Due to (1.1) and (5.45), we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \beta ={2\times 1.76\times 10^{-4}\times 69911\times 10^3\over 150} \cos (38^\circ )\approx 129< \beta _\mathrm{{crit}}(ay+b, d) \approx 1004. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}β=2×1.76×10-4×69911×103150cos(38∘)≈129<βcrit(ay+b,d)≈1004.By Theorem 5.10 and Remark 5.17, the rigidity of traveling waves holds in a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2-neighborhood of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_0,0)$$\end{document}(u0,0) for arbitrary wave speeds and zonal periods. This result is consistent with the absence of genuine traveling waves in the corresponding zonal band and provides theoretical support for the observed shear-flow patterns described in [39].

Under the Rayleigh stability condition, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2 shear flows (not necessarily monotone) turn out to be nonlinearly Lyapunov stable (see Theorem 5.19 below). However, due to the existence of genuine nearby traveling waves, asymptotic stability (i.e. nonlinear inviscid damping) fails near a monotone shear flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_0(y),0)$$\end{document}(u0(y),0) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\beta ,L)\in \bigg (\max \bigg \{(u_0'')_{\max },$$\end{document}(β,L)∈(max{(u0′′)max, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \lambda _1^{-1}(0,u_{0,\min })\bigg \},\infty \bigg )\times \left[ {2\pi \over \sqrt{-\lambda _1(\beta ,u_{0,\min })}},\infty \right) $$\end{document}λ1-1(0,u0,min)},∞)×2π-λ1(β,u0,min),∞. This means that in such case, the long-time dynamics near the monotone shear flow is considerably richer. The rigidity near the monotone shear flow suggests that asymptotic stability may potentially be proved in certain function spaces for other values of (β ,L)(β,L). In the f-plane setting, it is worth noting that nonlinear inviscid damping in certain Gevrey spaces has been proved near a class of monotone shear flows in [28, 37], where the Rayleigh stability condition does not hold since the shear flow \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0$$\end{document}u0 satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0''=0$$\end{document}u0′′=0 near the boundary and, thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0=\beta \in Ran(u_0'')$$\end{document}0=β∈Ran(u0′′).

Theorem 5.19

Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0\in C^2([-d,d])$$\end{document}u0∈C2([-d,d]), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0'\ne 0$$\end{document}u0′≠0 on [-d,d][-d,d], and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\beta ,L)\in (\max \{(u_0'')_{\max }, $$\end{document}(β,L)∈(max{(u0′′)max, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \lambda _1^{-1}(0,u_{0,\min })\},\infty )\times \left[ {2\pi \over \sqrt{-\lambda _1(\beta ,u_{0,\min })}},\infty \right) $$\end{document}λ1-1(0,u0,min)},∞)×2π-λ1(β,u0,min),∞. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_0, 0)$$\end{document}(u0,0) is nonlinearly Lyapunov stable in (vorticity) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2, but nonlinear inviscid damping fails near \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_0, 0)$$\end{document}(u0,0) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2.

Proof

First, we prove that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_0, 0)$$\end{document}(u0,0) is nonlinearly Lyapunov stable in (vorticity) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 as long as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \notin Ran(u_0'')$$\end{document}β∉Ran(u0′′). The proof of nonlinear Lyapunov stability is valid for general \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2 shear flows, not necessarily the monotone ones. We use the standard method introduced by Arnold [1, 2]. \begin{document}$$f=f_0$$\end{document}f=f0 on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Ran(\gamma _0+\beta y)$$\end{document}Ran(γ0+βy) and5.47\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0<12u0,min-cβ-(u0′′)min≤-f′≤2(u0,max-c)β-(u0′′)max.We choose F such that F'=fF′=f. Define the energy-Casimir-momentum functional by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} H(\gamma )=\int _{D_L}\left( -{1\over 2}|\vec {u}|^2-F(\gamma +\beta y)+cy(\gamma +\beta y)\right) dxdy \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H(γ)=∫DL-12|u→|2-F(γ+βy)+cy(γ+βy)dxdyalong a perturbed solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vec {u}=(u,v)$$\end{document}u→=(u,v) of the β β-plane equation. Indeed, if the circulation of the perturbed solution is different from that of the shear flow, then we can first modify the shear flow to a nearby shear flow such that the circulation of the modified shear flow is the same as that of the perturbed solution. Then, by an argument analogous to the one used below for nonlinear stability, we can show that the perturbed solution remains \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-close (in vorticity) to the modified shear flow for all t>0t>0. Note that the functional H(γ )H(γ) is conserved along the solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vec {u}=(u,v)$$\end{document}u→=(u,v). Let (X(s; x, y), Y(s; x, y)) be the particle trajectory along the velocity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vec {u}$$\end{document}u→, that is, (X(s; x, y), Y(s; x, y)) solves the equation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \dot{X}(s)=u(s,X(s), Y(s)),\\ \dot{Y}(s)=v(s,X(s), Y(s)), \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}X˙(s)=u(s,X(s),Y(s)),Y˙(s)=v(s,X(s),Y(s)),with the initial data X(0)=x,Y(0)=yX(0)=x,Y(0)=y. Note that (2.4) expresses the conservation of total vorticity along a fluid trajectory and the flow map (X, Y) is area preserving, that is,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {d\over dt}(\gamma (t,X(t),Y(t))+\beta Y(t))=0\quad \text {and} \quad {\partial {(X,Y)}\over \partial {(x,y)}}=1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddt(γ(t,X(t),Y(t))+βY(t))=0and∂(X,Y)∂(x,y)=1.Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&{d\over dt}\int _{D_L}F(\gamma +\beta y)dxdy\\&\quad =\int _{D_L}F'(\gamma (t,X(t),Y(t))+\beta Y(t)){d\over dt}(\gamma (t,X(t),Y(t))+\beta Y(t))dXdY=0\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddt∫DLF(γ+βy)dxdy=∫DLF′(γ(t,X(t),Y(t))+βY(t))ddt(γ(t,X(t),Y(t))+βY(t))dXdY=0,showing that the Casimir functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _{D_L}F(\gamma +\beta y) dxdy$$\end{document}∫DLF(γ+βy)dxdy is conserved along the solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vec {u}$$\end{document}u→.

Applications to the Couette–Poiseuille flow, to the Bickley Jet and to the Kolmogorov Flow

In this section, building on the results of Sect. 3, we investigate the rigidity of traveling waves near non-monotone shear flows which include the Couette–Poiseuille flow and the Bickley jet in a bounded channel. Moreover, we study the rigidity of traveling waves with a fixed wave speed near a class of shear flows and describe the inviscid dynamical structures near the Kolmogorov flow in the f-plane setting.

Rigidity Near a Class of Shear Flows with Applications

Based on Theorem 3.12, we establish a rigidity result for geophysical traveling waves with arbitrary speeds near a class of general shear flows. Then we apply this result to the Couette–Poiseuille flow and the Bickley jet.

A General Rigidity Result

We first present a rigidity criterion near a general class of shear flows.

Proposition 6.1

Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0\in C^2([-d,d])$$\end{document}u0∈C2([-d,d]), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_0'')_{\min }>0$$\end{document}(u0′′)min>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \in \left[ 0,(u_0'')_{\min }\right) $$\end{document}β∈0,(u0′′)min. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))\in C^2(D_L)$$\end{document}(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))∈C2(DL) is a traveling-wave solution to the β β-plane equation (2.1)-(2.2) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}c∈R, and satisfies6.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Delta u-u_{ 0}''\Vert _{C^0(D_L)}<(u_0'')_{\min }-\beta , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Δu-u0′′‖C0(DL)<(u0′′)min-β,then (u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y)) is a shear flow.

Proof

It follows from (6.1) that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |(\Delta u)(x,y)-u_{0}''(y)| <(u_{0}'')_{\min }-\beta ,\quad (x,y)\in D_L. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|(Δu)(x,y)-u0′′(y)|<(u0′′)min-β,(x,y)∈DL.Thus\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \beta -(u_{0}'')_{\min }<(\Delta u)(x,y)-u_{0}''(y)\le (\Delta u)(x,y)-(u_{0}'')_{\min },\quad (x,y)\in D_{L}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}β-(u0′′)min<(Δu)(x,y)-u0′′(y)≤(Δu)(x,y)-(u0′′)min,(x,y)∈DL.Hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le \beta <(\Delta u)_{\min }$$\end{document}0≤β<(Δu)min. By Theorem 3.12, (u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y)) is a shear flow. □ □

Example 6.2

Saturn’s southern polar troposphere features at cloud level a strongly sheared circumpolar jet flowing eastward in the zonal band between 65\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^\circ $$\end{document}∘S and 70\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^\circ $$\end{document}∘S. The jet profile exhibits a nearly flat slope at 65\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^\circ $$\end{document}∘S, with a poleward speed increasing from about \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\,\text {m}\,\text {s}^{-1}$$\end{document}0ms-1 at 65\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^\circ $$\end{document}∘S to a maximal speed of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$100\,\text {m}\,\text {s}^{-1}$$\end{document}100ms-1 at 70\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^\circ $$\end{document}∘S (see Fig. 12.5 in [42]). With the scaling parameters provided in Table 1, we obtain the boundary values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0(-d)={2\over 3}$$\end{document}u0(-d)=23, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0(d)=0$$\end{document}u0(d)=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0'(d)=0$$\end{document}u0′(d)=0. Consequently, we may model this sheared, purely zonal flow by the parabolic profile\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_0(y)={1\over 6d^2}y^2-{1\over 3d}y+{1\over 6},\quad -d\le y\le d, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u0(y)=16d2y2-13dy+16,-d≤y≤d,in a band of width \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2d={\pi \over 36}\approx 0.0873$$\end{document}2d=π36≈0.0873 corresponding to about 5 degrees of latitude. Using the data in Table 1, we infer from (1.1) that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \beta ={2\times 1.62\times 10^{-4}\times 58232\times 10^3\over 150}\cos (67.5^\circ )\approx 48<(u_0'')_{\min }={1\over 3d^2}\approx 175 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}β=2×1.62×10-4×58232×103150cos(67.5∘)≈48<(u0′′)min=13d2≈175so that Proposition 6.1 predicts the absence of genuine traveling waves near this zonal flow. This conclusion is validated by the field data (see [42]).

Application to the Couette–Poiseuille Flow

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}Γ∈R, the generalized Couette–Poiseuille flow is given by6.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_\textrm{cp}(y)=\Gamma y+(1-\Gamma )y^2, \quad y\in [-1,1]\,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ucp(y)=Γy+(1-Γ)y2,y∈[-1,1].

Corollary 6.3

Assume that Γ <1Γ<1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \in \left[ 0,2-2\Gamma \right) $$\end{document}β∈0,2-2Γ. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))\in C^2(D_L)$$\end{document}(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))∈C2(DL) is a traveling-wave solution to the β β-plane equation (2.1)-(2.2) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}c∈R and such that6.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Delta u-u_\textrm{cp}''\Vert _{C^0(D_L)}<2-2\Gamma -\beta , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Δu-ucp′′‖C0(DL)<2-2Γ-β,then (u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y)) is a shear flow in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_L=\mathbb {T}_L\times [-d,d]$$\end{document}DL=TL×[-d,d] with d=1d=1.

Proof

Since Γ <1Γ<1, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\textrm{cp}''\equiv 2-2\Gamma >0$$\end{document}ucp′′≡2-2Γ>0. The result therefore follows immediately from Proposition 6.1. □ □

Remark 6.4

For the Poiseuille flow, corresponding to Γ =0Γ=0 in (6.2), in the f-plane setting, Corollary 6.3 complements the results in [13, 15]. Indeed, the rigidity of traveling waves with arbitrary wave speeds was obtained in [13] under the high regularity assumption (velocity) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{>6}$$\end{document}H>6, and Corollary 6.3 lowers the regularity to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2. In addition, although the rigidity of steady flows near the Poiseuille flow was proved in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^2$$\end{document}C2 (see [15]), Corollary 6.3 further extends the conclusion to encompass all traveling wave speeds. See also the recent independent result for β =0β=0 in Corollary 1.6(i) of [18].

Application to the Bickley Jet

The profile of a bounded Bickley jet is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_\textrm{b}(y)=-\operatorname {sech}^{2}(y),\quad y\in [-d,d]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ub(y)=-sech2(y),y∈[-d,d].As another application of Proposition 6.1, we investigate the rigidity of traveling waves with arbitrary speeds near the Bickley jet.

Corollary 6.5

Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\in \left( 0,\frac{\ln (2+\sqrt{3})}{2}\right) $$\end{document}d∈0,ln(2+3)2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \in \left[ 0,2\operatorname {sech}^{4}(d)-4\operatorname {sech}^{2}\right. \left. (d)\operatorname {tanh}^{2}(d)\right) $$\end{document}β∈0,2sech4(d)-4sech2(d)tanh2(d). If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))\in C^2(D_L)$$\end{document}(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))∈C2(DL) is a traveling-wave solution to the β β-plane equation (2.1)-(2.2) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}c∈R, and satisfies6.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \Delta u-u_\textrm{b}''\Vert _{C^0(D_L)}<2\operatorname {sech}^{4}(d)-4\operatorname {sech}^{2}(d)\operatorname {tanh}^{2}(d)-\beta , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Δu-ub′′‖C0(DL)<2sech4(d)-4sech2(d)tanh2(d)-β,then (u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y)) is a shear flow.

Remark 6.6

The real zeros of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_\textrm{b}''(y)=2\operatorname {sech}^{4}(y)-4\operatorname {sech}^{2}(y)\operatorname {tanh}^{2}(y)=0$$\end{document}ub′′(y)=2sech4(y)-4sech2(y)tanh2(y)=0 are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\ln (2+\sqrt{3})}{2}$$\end{document}ln(2+3)2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\ln (2-\sqrt{3})}{2}$$\end{document}ln(2-3)2. Proof

By (6.5), we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_\textrm{b}'')_{\min }= 2\operatorname {sech}^{4}(d)-4\operatorname {sech}^{2}(d)\operatorname {tanh}^{2}(d)>0$$\end{document}(ub′′)min=2sech4(d)-4sech2(d)tanh2(d)>0. The conclusion follows directly from Proposition 6.1. □ □

Application to the f-Plane Setting

In this subsection, as an application of Theorem 3.3, we study the rigidity of traveling waves in the f-plane setting. Moreover, we prove some rigidity results for traveling waves with non-vanishing speeds near a Kolmogorov flow in the f-plane setting. On the other hand, we construct genuine analytic traveling waves with vanishing speed (i.e. steady flow) near the Kolmogorov flow.

Rigidity of Traveling Waves in the f-Plane Setting

The following rigidity result in the f-plane setting is a consequence of Theorem 3.3.

Corollary 6.7

Let β =0β=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}c∈R. Assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta u\ne 0$$\end{document}Δu≠0 whenever u=cu=c. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))\in C^2(D_L)$$\end{document}(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))∈C2(DL) is a traveling-wave solution to the β β-plane equation (2.1)-(2.2), then (u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y)) is a shear flow.

Remark 6.8

In the f-plane setting, by employing a different approach, Corollary 6.7 generalizes Corollary 1.6 \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textrm{ii})$$\end{document}(ii) in [18] (for the case of a bounded periodic channel) and Theorem 1.2 in [15] in two aspects: one is by generalizing steady flows to traveling waves with non-vanishing wave speeds, and the other aspect is that the traveling waves are not necessarily near a shear flow. See Proposition 6.9 for the rigidity of traveling waves with a fixed wave speed near a shear flow.

As an application of Corollary 6.7, we establish the rigidity of traveling waves with a fixed wave speed near a class of shear flows in the f-plane setting.

Proposition 6.9

Let β =0β=0. Applying Corollary 6.7, (u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y)) is a shear flow. □ □

The following remark about the f-plane setting explains how Theorem 3.3 (ii) complements the rigidity result established in [20]:

Remark 6.10

In the f-plane setting, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))\in C^2(D_L)$$\end{document}(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))∈C2(DL) be a traveling-wave solution to (2.1)-(2.2). Then (u-c,v)(u-c,v) is a steady solution of (2.1)-(2.2). Theorem 3.3 (ii) can be viewed as a rigidity result in the presence of stagnation points of (u-c,v)(u-c,v), see (3.10). This stands in contrast to the rigidity result by Hamel-Nadirashvili [20], which assumes that (u-c,v)(u-c,v) has no stagnation points throughout the domain. Our result therefore complements Hamel-Nadirashvili’s by addressing a regime that was previously excluded. The combination of Theorem 3.3 (ii) and Hamel-Nadirashvili’s result can be stated as “if c is not a generalized inflection value of u or if (u-c,v)(u-c,v) has no stagnation points, then (u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y))(u(x-ct,y),v(x-ct,y)) is a shear flow in the f-plane setting."

Application to Inviscid Dynamical Structures Near the Kolmogorov Flow in the f-Plane Setting

In the finite channel \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,y)\in D_{L_0}=\mathbb {T}_{L_0}\times [-d,d]$$\end{document}(x,y)∈DL0=TL0×[-d,d] with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_0={4\pi \over \sqrt{3}}$$\end{document}L0=4π3 and d=π d=π, we consider the Kolmogorov flow6.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_\textrm{k}(y)=\sin (y),\quad y\in [-\pi ,\pi ]\,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}uk(y)=sin(y),y∈[-π,π],and we study the inviscid dynamical structures near it in the f-plane setting.

Proposition 6.11

Let β =0β=0.
